M7  (Medium)  [DONE]
===============================================================================
[Adjudicated final brief]

Root cause: In `lib/supabase/client.ts:11` the Supabase auth client is configured with `storage: AsyncStorage`. AsyncStorage is unencrypted plaintext at rest (an SQLite/plist file in the app sandbox). Because `persistSession: true` and `autoRefreshToken: true`, Supabase writes the full session — the short-lived access JWT AND the long-lived refresh token — into that store under key `sb-<project-ref>-auth-token`. A refresh token is bearer-equivalent and long-lived, so an attacker with filesystem/backup access (jailbreak, unencrypted iTunes/Finder backup, forensic extraction) can lift it and mint sessions. `expo-secure-store` was never added as a dependency, so the app never had a Keychain-backed option.

Fix: Move the persisted Supabase session from plaintext AsyncStorage into the iOS Keychain via expo-secure-store, through a size-safe chunking adapter that (a) is scoped to native platforms only via Platform.OS (AsyncStorage remains on web, which has no SecureStore implementation), (b) clears stale chunk keys on every write so no orphaned fragments of a rotated token remain, and (c) REQUIRES a one-time migration/deletion of the legacy plaintext AsyncStorage session on first run after the update, so existing installs are not left exposed indefinitely (this was optional in the original design; promoted to required because it is the only step that fixes the root cause for the existing user base).

Files to change:
  - c:\Users\aadik\Downloads\App\lib\supabase\client.ts
      Import the new secure storage adapter and Platform from react-native. Replace `storage: AsyncStorage` (line 11) with `storage: Platform.OS === 'web' ? AsyncStorage : secureStorageAdapter`, so the web dev target (package.json 'web' script, app.json web.bundler config, react-native-web dependency) keeps working — expo-secure-store has no functional web implementation. Keep the AsyncStorage import (still used on web). Also add (or call from AuthContext init) the required one-time migration: read legacy AsyncStorage key `sb-<project-ref>-auth-token`; if present, copy into secureStorageAdapter (preferred) or delete it outright, guarded by a sentinel flag so it runs once and no-ops on fresh installs.
  - c:\Users\aadik\Downloads\App\lib\supabase\secureStorage.ts
      NEW small file. Export a storage object with async getItem/setItem/removeItem backed by `expo-secure-store`, using AFTER_FIRST_UNLOCK accessibility. Chunk any value exceeding ~2000 bytes across `<key>.<n>` entries plus a `<key>.count` meta entry. setItem MUST first read the existing `<key>.count` (if any) and delete all prior `<key>.0..oldCount-1` chunk entries before writing the new chunk set and new `.count` — prevents orphaned fragments of a previous (larger) session persisting after a smaller session overwrites it. Write order within setItem: write all new chunk entries, then write `.count` last, so a mid-write crash fails safe into 'no valid session' (forced re-login) rather than a corrupted partial blob. getItem reassembles by count; removeItem deletes all chunks plus the count key. Keep it under ~50 lines, with the one-line-comment-above-each-function convention from CLAUDE.md.
  - c:\Users\aadik\Downloads\App\package.json
      Add `expo-secure-store` to dependencies (install via `npx expo install expo-secure-store` so the SDK-54-compatible version is pinned). No app.json plugins-array entry is needed — confirmed by precedent: `expo-camera` is a dependency not listed in app.json's plugins array and still autolinks; expo-secure-store's optional config plugin only sets NSFaceIDUsageDescription, which this fix does not need (AFTER_FIRST_UNLOCK, no Face ID).
  - c:\Users\aadik\Downloads\App\jest.setup.js
      Add a global `jest.mock('expo-secure-store', ...)` (in-memory map implementing getItemAsync/setItemAsync/deleteItemAsync), following the same pattern and comment style as the existing AsyncStorage mock. Required because `foodDB.test.ts` does not mock `@/lib/supabase/client` directly and would otherwise transitively load the real `secureStorage.ts` -> `expo-secure-store` and hit the null-native-module failure the existing AsyncStorage mock's comment already warns about.
  - c:\Users\aadik\Downloads\App\lib\supabase\__tests__\secureStorage.test.ts
      NEW test file. Mock `expo-secure-store` with an in-memory map; assert: (1) set→get round-trip for a small value; (2) a >2048-byte value is split into multiple keychain entries and reassembled identically; (3) removeItem clears every chunk (no orphaned `.n` keys); (4) overwrite-shrink case — write a large multi-chunk value, then overwrite the same key with a smaller value, and assert no stale `<key>.<n>` entries beyond the new count remain in the mock store.

Blast radius & safety:
  Callers:    `lib/supabase/client.ts` exports the singleton `supabase`. Verified importer count (grep for 'supabase/client'): 8 non-test source files (foodDB.ts, openAI.ts, Connector.ts, accountFunctions.tsx, authFunctions.tsx, AuthContext/index.tsx, app/settingsScreens/support.tsx, components/devTest/DevStatsModal.tsx) plus 4 test files (authFunctions.test.ts, accountFunctions.test.ts, connector.test.ts, foodDB.test.ts) — 12 total, not the originally-stated 28 (which miscounted docs/graphify-out artifacts as importers). None of the 8 source callers touch the `auth.storage` adapter directly — they call `supabase.auth.*` and edge functions, which go through Supabase's internal session handling. The behavioral surface that changes is WHERE the session bytes are persisted on native, plus a one-time migration effect on first launch; web is explicitly excluded via the Platform.OS branch.
  Edge cases: (1) Value >2048 bytes — handled by the chunking adapter. (2) Overwrite-shrink — a smaller session must not leave stale higher-index chunks from a prior larger write; setItem now clears the old chunk set before writing (amended — was missing from the original design). (3) Existing installed users — REQUIRED migration/delete of the legacy AsyncStorage key on first run after update (amended from 'optional' — without it the root cause remains unfixed for the existing user base); worst case if migration copy fails is a forced Apple Sign-In re-auth (a single native tap), acceptable for a Medium fix. (4) Web target — `expo start --web` (package.json), the metro web bundler (app.json web.*), and react-native-web are active; expo-secure-store has no web implementation, so storage now branches on Platform.OS to keep AsyncStorage on web (amended — the original design would have broken the web dev target). (5) Background token refresh — AFTER_FIRST_UNLOCK accessibility keeps the Keychain readable when `autoRefreshToken` fires while backgrounded; the stricter WHEN_UNLOCKED default would break refresh. (6) Non-atomic multi-key write — a crash mid-write between chunk entries and the `.count` meta could yield an inconsistent session; write order (chunks first, count last) makes this fail safe into 'no session' (forced re-login), the same accepted failure class as edge case (3)'s fallback, not a new severity tier. (7) Keychain items persist across app uninstall/reinstall, unlike AsyncStorage which is wiped on delete — with AFTER_FIRST_UNLOCK and no device-only scoping, a reinstalled app will silently retain the prior session. This is a genuine, disclosed behavior change (new to this brief) and should be a deliberate, informed tradeoff rather than a silent side effect; no code change is mandated for it given the complexity a reinstall-detection heuristic would add for a Medium, non-blocking fix. (8) Offline/first-run — getItem returning null is already the unauthenticated path the app handles today.
  Preserved:  Sign-out (`supabase.auth.signOut()`) and account deletion still route session clearing through the adapter's `removeItem`, which removes all chunk keys, so no plaintext token fragment is left behind. Edge-function auth headers are derived from the in-memory session, untouched by the storage change. `userStorage.clearUserStorage` continues to manage only its own non-sensitive keys (`lastExercise:<userID>`) and is unaffected — the legacy Supabase auth-token key cleanup is handled by the new required migration step, not by `clearUserStorage`.
  Tests:      Add `lib/supabase/__tests__/secureStorage.test.ts` (round-trip + chunking + full-cleanup on remove + overwrite-shrink cleanup, with expo-secure-store mocked). Add a global `expo-secure-store` mock to `jest.setup.js` so `foodDB.test.ts` and any other test that transitively loads `client.ts` do not hit the null-native-module failure. Run `npm run test:ci` to confirm existing AuthContext tests (`authFunctions.test.ts`, `accountFunctions.test.ts`) and `connector.test.ts` still pass with their direct `supabase/client` mocks unaffected. Manual device check on a real EAS build: sign in on the pre-update build, install the updated build over it, relaunch -> confirm migration preserves the session (or gracefully forces one re-login) and that no `sb-*-auth-token` value remains in plaintext AsyncStorage afterward; separately verify `expo start --web` still authenticates correctly via AsyncStorage.
  DevHub:     approval=na; plan={"applicable":false,"naReason":"Backend/security storage-layer change (Supabase auth persistence adapter) with no isolated UI component to preview in Dev Hub."}

Adjudication decisions:
  - app.json plugins-array entry for expo-secure-store is unnecessary — expo-secure-store's config plugin only sets NSFaceIDUsageDescription for biometric-gated items, which this fix does not use (AFTER_FIRST_UNLOCK, no Face ID). Precedent: expo-camera is a dependency (package.json:30) but is absent from app.json's plugins array (app.json:32-67) and still autolinks fine.
      -> ACCEPTED. Verified app.json:32-67 — expo-camera is indeed a dependency not listed in plugins, confirming autolinking works without a plugin entry for simple native modules. Drop the app.json file/plugin change from the brief; expo-secure-store will autolink via expo-modules-autolinking without any plugins-array entry since no Face ID prompt is being configured.  (Rule 4 (least code) — the plugin entry is an unnecessary file change with no functional benefit for this fix's configuration.)
  - Blast-radius count '28 files' is fabricated/overstated; real importer count is far lower.
      -> ACCEPTED. Grep for 'supabase/client' returns 17 matches total; excluding docs (PRODUCTION_READINESS_*.txt/.md) and graphify-out artifacts (graph.html/json), the real count is 8 non-test source files (foodDB.ts, openAI.ts, Connector.ts, accountFunctions.tsx, authFunctions.tsx, index.tsx, support.tsx, DevStatsModal.tsx) plus 4 test files (authFunctions.test.ts, accountFunctions.test.ts, connector.test.ts, foodDB.test.ts) = 12, not 28. The qualitative claim ('none touch auth.storage directly') still holds and is unaffected. Amend the blast-radius text to state the accurate count.  (Rule 2 (safety/blast radius) — an inflated count undermines confidence in the blast-radius analysis; correct it, though it does not change the substantive safety conclusion.)
  - Chunking adapter does not clear stale higher-index chunks when a new session write is shorter than the previous one, leaving orphaned fragments of the prior (possibly rotated) refresh token as residual plaintext-in-Keychain data.
      -> ACCEPTED and the fix design is amended. This directly undermines the security property the fix exists to deliver (minimizing sensitive bytes at rest). secureStorage.ts's setItem must, before writing new chunks, delete any pre-existing chunk key at index >= new chunk count (or simplest: always delete the full previous chunk set — read the old `<key>.count`, remove `<key>.0..oldCount-1` and `<key>.count` — then write the new chunks and new count). Add a regression test: write a large multi-chunk value, then overwrite with a smaller value, and assert no `<key>.<n>` keys beyond the new count remain in the mock store.  (Rule 1 (correctness, HARD GATE) — the fix must actually eliminate the plaintext-at-rest exposure it targets; leaving orphaned old-token chunks behind is an incomplete fix for the exact vulnerability class in scope.)
  - Unconditional swap to expo-secure-store breaks the web target — SecureStore has no functional web implementation (throws/rejects), while the app has an active web dev config (app.json web.bundler, package.json 'web' script, react-native-web dependency).
      -> ACCEPTED. Verified package.json:9 has `"web": "expo start --web"`, app.json:27-31 configures the metro web bundler, and react-native-web is a dependency (package.json:64). Amend client.ts's storage config to branch on `Platform.OS`: use the new secureStorageAdapter on ios/android, and keep AsyncStorage on web. This is a small, additive change (one Platform.OS check) that fully closes the regression without touching non-web behavior.  (Rule 1 (correctness, HARD GATE) combined with Rule 2 (safety) — the brief's own claim that 'the only behavioral surface that changes is WHERE the session bytes are persisted' is falsified for web; an unguarded swap is a real regression on an actively configured target, not a hypothetical.)
  - Root cause is not resolved for the existing installed user base — the brief marks the copy-then-delete migration as OPTIONAL, but without it the plaintext refresh token already on disk under the old AsyncStorage key is never read, never migrated, and never deleted, so it lingers indefinitely on every upgrading device.
      -> ACCEPTED and promoted from optional to REQUIRED. This is the single most material finding: M7's root cause is 'plaintext bearer-equivalent refresh token at rest,' and skipping migration leaves that exact plaintext token in place for the entire existing user base — the fix would ship without fixing the vulnerability for anyone who upgrades rather than fresh-installs. The brief is amended to require, on first launch after the update, a one-time step that (a) reads the legacy AsyncStorage key (`sb-<project-ref>-auth-token`), (b) if present, either copies it into the new secure adapter (preserving the session, preferred for UX) or at minimum deletes it outright (accepting forced re-login) — either way the stale plaintext must not survive. This can live as a small one-time effect in client.ts or AuthContext init, guarded so it runs once (e.g. via a sentinel flag) and is a no-op on fresh installs where the legacy key never existed.  (Rule 1 (correctness, HARD GATE) — a fix that resolves the vulnerability only for new installs while leaving it fully unmitigated for the existing (larger) installed base does not fully fix the issue; per the rubric this loses regardless of the rest of the design's elegance.)
  - iOS Keychain persists across app uninstall/reinstall, unlike AsyncStorage which is wiped on delete; with AFTER_FIRST_UNLOCK and no device-only scoping, a reinstalled app silently retains the prior session — a behavior change the blast-radius analysis omits.
      -> ACCEPTED as a disclosure requirement, not a mandatory code change. This is a real, verifiable semantic change (Keychain items are not removed by app deletion) but forcing keychain-clear-on-reinstall detection adds meaningful complexity (a 'first launch after install' heuristic has its own edge cases with device backups) for a Medium-severity, non-launch-blocking fix. Add it explicitly to the brief's edgeCases so the change is disclosed and can be a deliberate, informed tradeoff (a persisted login across reinstall is arguably a UX improvement, not obviously a regression) rather than a silent omission.  (Rule 2 (safety/blast radius) — must be disclosed for an informed decision, but does not rise to a correctness gate since it is a plausible, arguably-desirable behavior change rather than a broken caller.)
  - The chunked multi-key write replaces AsyncStorage's atomic single-key write with a non-atomic multi-key write; a crash between writing chunks and the `.count` meta leaves count inconsistent with stored chunks, producing a corrupted/unreadable session on next getItem.
      -> PARTIALLY ACCEPTED, folded into an existing accepted risk rather than a new blocking requirement. The brief already accepts, in edgeCases, that a swap-related failure mode results in a forced Apple Sign-In re-auth ("a single native tap") as tolerable for a Medium fix. A rare write-order crash producing the same forced-re-login outcome is the same accepted failure class, not a new severity tier — it degrades gracefully rather than corrupting data or leaking anything. Amend the spec only to state the safe write order explicitly (write all chunk entries first, write `.count` last, so a mid-write crash yields a stale-but-consistent-or-missing count that fails safe into 'no session' rather than a partially-overwritten blob), and add one sentence noting this to edgeCases. No separate file or test is required beyond what rule 1's chunking-overwrite fix already touches.  (Rule 4 (least code) — the residual risk is already covered by an accepted tradeoff elsewhere in the brief; solving it fully (e.g., a write-ahead log) would be disproportionate to a Medium-severity fix, so a one-line spec clarification is the proportionate response.)
  - No global expo-secure-store jest mock exists; `foodDB.test.ts` require()s the real `foodDB.ts`, which imports the real `supabase/client.ts`, which will import the new `secureStorage.ts` -> `expo-secure-store`, and `jest.setup.js` only mocks `@react-native-async-storage/async-storage` — risking a null-native-module failure in that (and any other unmocked) test that transitively loads the client.
      -> ACCEPTED. Verified: `lib/foodDB/foodDB.ts:2` imports `{ supabase } from '@/lib/supabase/client'`, and `lib/foodDB/__tests__/foodDB.test.ts` contains no `jest.mock('@/lib/supabase/client', ...)` (unlike `authFunctions.test.ts`, `accountFunctions.test.ts`, and `connector.test.ts`, which do mock it directly). `jest.setup.js` currently registers only the AsyncStorage mock. Add a matching global mock for `expo-secure-store` to `jest.setup.js` (same pattern/comment style as the existing AsyncStorage mock) so any test that transitively loads `client.ts` resolves the new native dependency safely, rather than requiring every current and future test file to mock it individually.  (Rule 2 (safety/blast radius) — the fix must not break existing passing tests; Rule 3 (convention fit) — extend the established jest.setup.js global-mock pattern rather than scattering ad hoc mocks.)

M8  (Medium)  [DONE]
===============================================================================
[Adjudicated final brief]

Root cause: Two distinct, previously-conflated defects in the AI photo/text analysis path: (1) No cancellation propagation. When the user cancels the analyze modal, analyzingModal.tsx's beforeRemove listener (line 32-34) only sets canceledRef.current=true, and analyzePhoto (line 86, 90) uses that purely to discard the already-in-flight result client-side — it never aborts the underlying network call. lib/openAI/openAI.ts:22 (callEdgeFunction) issues its fetch to the Edge Function with no AbortController/signal, so the client connection stays open. The Edge Function's serve handler (index.ts:162) never reads req.signal (the standard signal that fires on client disconnect), and its own provider fetches (index.ts:93 OpenAI, index.ts:138 Gemini) have no abort wiring either. Since a healthy OpenAI/Gemini call normally completes in a few seconds, a cancelled scan bills in full before any fixed deadline could help. (2) No upper bound for a hung/slow provider, independent of cancellation — if OpenAI/Gemini hangs, the Edge Function's fetch has no deadline, so Edge Function wall-clock exposure (and the risk of an uncaught exception surfacing as a raw 500 instead of the normalized CallResult error shape) is unbounded. Note: the client already races a separate 30s withTimeout (context/NutritionContext/functions/aiFunctions.tsx:93,160) that fails the UI at 30s regardless of any server-side change — this fix does not alter that user-visible behavior; it targets provider-cost/Edge-Function exposure and error-shape cleanliness. Label/item mode forcing detail:'high' (index.ts:120) makes an abandoned label scan the costliest instance of defect (1).

Fix: Two coordinated changes, both reusing standard Deno/Web APIs (no new deps/files): (a) lib/openAI/openAI.ts — callEdgeFunction: create an AbortController and pass controller.signal into the fetch(EDGE_FN_URL, {...}) call; wire the controller's abort() to fire when the analyze flow is cancelled (thread the existing cancel intent — e.g. canceledRef / the shouldCommit callback already passed to handleAnalyzeAndAddPhoto — through to this new controller). This is the missing client-side half of cancellation propagation: aborting the client fetch closes the connection, which the Edge Function can observe. (b) lib/supabase/functions/fetchOpenAI/index.ts — in the serve handler, build one merged signal per request: const signal = AbortSignal.any([req.signal, AbortSignal.timeout(PROVIDER_TIMEOUT_MS)]) with PROVIDER_TIMEOUT_MS = 30000 (matching the UI's stated window), and pass { signal } into both the OpenAI fetch (line 93) and the Gemini fetch (line 138). This replaces the previously-proposed manual AbortController+setTimeout+clearTimeout helper: it is less code, and because the signal stays armed for the fetch's full lifecycle (not cleared after headers arrive) it also bounds a slow/hung body read at res.json() (lines 100, 148), which the manual-timer design would not have covered. Wrap each provider call in try/catch so an AbortError (from either the real client-cancel path or the 30s ceiling) is normalized — the timeout branch returns the existing CallResult error shape (e.g. { error: { status: 504, body: { error: 'timeout' } } }), and a genuine client-cancel abort exits cleanly without throwing (there's no client left to read a response). Deliberately still do NOT touch the rate-limit/quota gap (separate issue), the prompts, or provider selection; and do NOT attempt to convert the provider calls to streaming — that would be needed to reliably stop upstream token generation/billing mid-flight for a non-streaming completion, and is an out-of-scope, materially larger change. This residual limitation (abort does not guarantee the provider itself halts generation/billing for a non-streaming request) is accepted and documented, not silently overclaimed.

Files to change:
  - lib/supabase/functions/fetchOpenAI/index.ts
      Add PROVIDER_TIMEOUT_MS = 30000. In the serve handler, build signal = AbortSignal.any([req.signal, AbortSignal.timeout(PROVIDER_TIMEOUT_MS)]) and pass { signal } into the fetch calls at line 93 (OpenAI) and line 138 (Gemini). Wrap each provider call in try/catch to normalize AbortError into the existing CallResult error shape (504 timeout, or clean exit on a genuine client-cancel) instead of an uncaught exception.
  - lib/openAI/openAI.ts
      In callEdgeFunction (line 12-48), create an AbortController and pass its signal into the fetch(EDGE_FN_URL, {...}) call at line 22; expose a way for the analyze flow to trigger abort() on cancel so the client half of cancellation propagation actually exists.
  - app/nutritionScreens/analyzingModal.tsx / context/NutritionContext/functions/aiFunctions.tsx
      Thread the existing cancel signal (canceledRef / beforeRemove at analyzingModal.tsx:32-34) through handleAnalyzeAndAddPhoto down to callEdgeFunction's new AbortController so cancelling the modal actually aborts the in-flight request, instead of only discarding the result at analyzingModal.tsx:86,90. No change to the existing client-side withTimeout(...,30000) race in aiFunctions.tsx:93,160 — it is preserved as-is.

Blast radius & safety:
  Callers:    Every AI path still funnels through callOpenAI/callGeminiVision -> askOpenAIVision/askOpenAIText -> callEdgeFunction -> handleAnalyzeAndAddPhoto. The wrap must keep returning a CallResult (server side) and a resolved/rejected promise consistent with today's contract (client side) on the happy path, so toResponse and existing .catch handling in aiFunctions.tsx/analyzingModal.tsx are unaffected when nothing is cancelled and no timeout fires. Need to confirm no other caller of callEdgeFunction exists that would be broken by adding an AbortController parameter/plumbing.
  Edge cases: Verify AbortSignal.any and AbortSignal.timeout are supported in the deployed Supabase Edge Function Deno runtime version before relying on them (call out as an explicit pre-merge check, since the original brief assumed only AbortController/setTimeout, which are unquestionably supported). Provider responding just before the deadline must not produce a late/stray abort. A genuine client cancel must not surface as an Alert to the user (analyzePhoto's catch already checks canceledRef.current before alerting, line 90 — preserve that guard so an intentional abort doesn't show 'Analysis Failed'). Non-streaming provider billing may not be reduced by the abort even when it fires — documented residual limitation, not a regression, since streaming migration is out of scope. AbortError must be caught server-side to avoid an uncaught exception/leaky stack (also a minor security concern, per original brief).
  Preserved:  Happy-path response shape, res.ok handling, and success latency under 30s are all unchanged. The pre-existing client-side 30s withTimeout race in aiFunctions.tsx (untouched) still governs user-visible wait time; this fix changes only provider-cost/Edge-Function exposure and error-normalization behavior on the cancel and hung-provider paths.
  Tests:      Deno test stubbing fetch with a never-resolving promise, asserting the 30s-ceiling branch returns the 504 CallResult; a second test asserting a fast-resolving fetch is unaffected (no stray abort, normal content returned). A new test/manual check confirming that when the client aborts mid-request (simulating modal cancel), the Edge Function exits cleanly without throwing and does not continue billing-relevant work past the abort. Manual regression: cancel a real scan mid-flight and confirm (via Edge Function logs) the invocation stops rather than completing; re-run the normal meal/item/label/text analyze flows end-to-end to confirm they still succeed unchanged.

Adjudication decisions:
  - Root-cause/fix mismatch (findings on index.ts:93, index.ts:138, analyzingModal.tsx:33/86/90, openAI.ts:22): a 30s Edge-Function-side timer cannot reduce billing for the primary scenario the brief leads with — a user cancelling the analyze modal. Verified: analyzingModal.tsx's beforeRemove listener (line 32-34) only sets canceledRef.current=true, and analyzePhoto (line 86, 90) uses it purely to discard the already-in-flight result client-side; it never aborts anything. lib/openAI/openAI.ts:22 callEdgeFunction's fetch has no AbortController/signal, so the client's HTTP connection to the Edge Function stays open on cancel. The Edge Function's own provider fetches (index.ts:93, 138) have no abort wiring either. A healthy OpenAI/Gemini call normally completes in a few seconds, so it bills fully long before any 30s ceiling could matter.
      -> MATERIAL, correctness gate failure. Amended the fix: it must include actual end-to-end cancellation propagation (client AbortController wired to the existing cancel intent, threaded into the Edge Function via the request connection), not just a fixed 30s timer. The 30s timer alone is retained as the secondary protection for a genuinely hung provider, but is no longer presented as solving the cancel-billing scenario.  (Correctness (rule 1, hard gate) — a fix that leaves the primary described defect unresolved loses regardless of the rest of its design.)
  - req.signal ignored as the natural cancellation channel (index.ts:162 serve handler never reads req.signal, which fires on client disconnect); a materially simpler/more correct approach threads req.signal (merged with a fixed ceiling) into the provider fetches instead of an independent blind timer.
      -> MATERIAL, adopted. Final fix passes AbortSignal.any([req.signal, AbortSignal.timeout(PROVIDER_TIMEOUT_MS)]) as the signal for both the OpenAI (line 93) and Gemini (line 138) fetch calls, so a real client disconnect/abort (once the client side is also fixed per the prior finding) and the 30s ceiling share one code path.  (Correctness (rule 1) driving the mechanism choice; also serves convention fit (rule 3, standard Web/Deno abort-signal composition) and least code (rule 4, avoids parallel bespoke plumbing).)
  - Client already has a 30s timeout (context/NutritionContext/functions/aiFunctions.tsx:93,160, withTimeout(...,30000) Promise.race for both vision and text calls) that the brief's rootCause/blastRadius never cites, so the added server timer's marginal user-facing benefit is nil and the brief's framing of a '30 second window' benefit is misleading.
      -> MATERIAL as an accuracy issue in the brief, not a defect in the underlying idea of bounding Edge Function wall-clock time. Amended rootCause/edgeCases text to explicitly acknowledge the existing client-side withTimeout race, and reframed the timer's purpose as bounding Edge Function compute/wall-clock exposure and producing a clean error shape for a hung provider — not as changing user-visible latency, which the client timeout already governs.  (Correctness/accuracy of the brief's own problem framing (supports rule 1); no code scope change required.)
  - Non-streaming billing overstated: neither the OpenAI request (index.ts:96, no stream flag) nor the Gemini request (index.ts:141-144, generateContent non-streaming) streams, so aborting the client-side fetch may not stop the provider from finishing generation and billing for it server-side; 'caps provider billing at ~30s' and 'abandoned scan bills less' overstate the guarantee.
      -> MATERIAL but not fully fixable within reasonable blast radius — closing this gap fully would require migrating both providers to streaming completions, a materially larger change with its own parsing/blast-radius implications, out of scope for a cost-containment fix. Softened the brief's claim: the fix bounds Edge Function wall-clock exposure and stops the client/edge function from waiting on / paying for further processing after the deadline, but does not guarantee the upstream provider halts token generation mid-request for a non-streaming call. Documented as an accepted residual limitation, not a blocking defect.  (Correctness gate honesty (rule 1, brief must not overclaim) balanced against safety/blast-radius (rule 2) and least code (rule 4) — do not take on a streaming migration to close a partial, honestly-disclosed gap.)
  - Body-read gap: the brief's originally proposed manual AbortController + setTimeout + clearTimeout(finally) helper only bounds the wait for fetch() to resolve (response headers). Once headers arrive the timer is cleared, so the subsequent res.json() body read (index.ts:100 OpenAI, index.ts:148 Gemini) is unbounded — a provider that returns headers fast but stalls mid-body evades the cap, contradicting the 'bounds a hung provider' claim.
      -> MATERIAL, adopted. Replaced the hand-rolled helper design with AbortSignal.timeout(PROVIDER_TIMEOUT_MS) / AbortSignal.any(...) passed directly as the fetch `signal`, which stays armed for the entire fetch lifecycle (headers + body read) rather than being cleared early — closing the body-read gap without extra code.  (Correctness (rule 1, closes a real unbounded-wait gap) and least code (rule 4, standard signal composition replaces a bespoke helper with more surface area).)
  - Alternative approach proposed directly: pass signal: AbortSignal.timeout(PROVIDER_TIMEOUT_MS) (a Deno/Web standard global) into each existing fetch, removing the manual helper entirely.
      -> MATERIAL, adopted (combined with req.signal per the second finding above into AbortSignal.any([...])). This is strictly simpler than the originally authored helper and closes the correctness gaps identified without adding a new file or dependency.  (Least code (rule 4) and convention fit (rule 3) — reuses a standard runtime primitive instead of hand-rolled timer bookkeeping; also required by rule 1 to close the body-read gap.)

[Adjudicated final brief]

Root cause: CORRECTED: the audit was NOT written before the pipeline landed — git history shows lib/openAI/mealImage.ts (with its resize/compress caps) was added in commit 01084b8 on 2026-06-26, three weeks BEFORE docs/PRODUCTION_READINESS_AUDIT.md was first committed in ec60886 on 2026-07-18; cameraScreen.tsx's takePicture/pickFromLibrary already called processCameraCapture/processPickedImageUri at that commit, and the audit's own M9 citation (cameraScreen.tsx:100-102,130-134; aiFunctions.tsx:91-93) points at exactly those call sites. So the auditor saw the resize pipeline and still flagged 'no dimension cap' — most plausibly because TEXT_IMAGE_MAX_WIDTH=1400 doesn't meet the audit's own suggested ≤1024px target, and/or the auditor didn't trace values into mealImage.ts. The substantive, verified facts: every capture path (meal 800px/0.8 compress, item/label 1400px/0.9 compress, both JPEG) is resized+compressed before base64 in aiFunctions.tsx:92, so the base64 string is meaningfully smaller than the audit's original ~3-5MB estimate for ANY photo taken through the app's real UI paths — but the resize op itself (`{ resize: { width } }`, expo-image-manipulator) only bounds width, not height or total megapixels, and it upscales rather than skips small sources. The true pixel-count bound in production is an IMPLICIT dependency on: (a) expo-image-picker's documented iOS behavior that allowsEditing:true always yields a square crop (library picks), (b) the camera sensor's fixed aspect ratio plus the meal-mode 3:4 SCAN_FRAME crop (capture). Item/label capture is intentionally uncropped for OCR legibility, so its payload (~1-2.5MB raw / ~1.3-3.3MB base64 plausible for a detail-dense label) is the least-tightly-bounded and highest-residual path, though still well under the audit's original 3-5MB figure. The audit's second stated concern — base64 allocated synchronously on the JS thread (aiFunctions.tsx:92) — is not a separate live risk: it happens entirely inside the already-visible analyzingModal loading UI that owns the ~30s OpenAI wait (per project architecture), so it doesn't cost any perceived responsiveness, not because the encode itself is 'negligible' in isolation.

Fix: Do NOT change the resize values (800/1400) or crop logic — MEAL_IMAGE_MAX_WIDTH=800 is deliberately cheap for recognition, TEXT_IMAGE_MAX_WIDTH=1400 is deliberately high-res for OCR legibility (mealImage.ts:23,27 comments); dropping either would be a correctness/UX regression, not a perf win, and is unjustified without a measured, reproducible jank/payload complaint. The correct, smallest change is a regression-guard test plus an honest documentation correction: (1) add lib/openAI/__tests__/mealImage.test.ts mocking expo-image-manipulator and asserting BOTH the resize width ceiling (≤800 meal, ≤1400 item/label) AND the exact compress values (0.8 / 0.9) and JPEG format for processPickedImageUri and processCameraCapture, plus that meal capture includes the crop op ahead of resize while item/label do not; (2) add one narrow assertion (in this file or a small cameraScreen-adjacent test) that app/nutritionScreens/cameraScreen.tsx's launchImageLibraryAsync call still passes allowsEditing:true — this is the real (implicit) pixel-count bound for library picks and must not silently regress; (3) correct the audit paper trail: annotate docs/PRODUCTION_READINESS_AUDIT.md:116 by its actual heading ('AI photos upload full-size') — not a literal 'M9' string, which does not appear in that file — noting the dimension cap already exists, applies before base64, and residual risk is downgraded but NOT to a uniform 'Low': item/label remains the higher-residual mode since 1400px exceeds the audit's own 1024px suggestion; also update docs/PRODUCTION_READINESS_INDEX.txt:203, where the 'M9' identifier actually lives, and docs/PRODUCTION_READINESS_FIXES.txt's M9 entry. Explicitly NOT touched: the base64 step at aiFunctions.tsx:92 (already shielded behind analyzingModal's loading UI), the edge-function JSON/base64 transport contract (multipart would shave the 33% inflation but is large-blast-radius for a payload this size), and quality:0.8 on the raw camera/library grab (feeds the resizer, harmless).

M10  (Medium)  [DONE]

[Adjudicated final brief]

Root cause: Graph1.tsx does three expensive things on every render/remount of the line chart, with cost scaling with account age because the body-weight series is dense-daily (up to 365 points at 1Y): (1) an O(n) chartKey string (every day:value pair, Graph1.tsx:39) and (2) an O(n) yScaleInfo computation (spread Math.min/max + nice-step loop, lines 74-103) both recompute on EVERY render of Graph1 — including incidental re-renders from chart-press-drag state updates, containerWidth onLayout, and font-readiness churn — not just when data actually changes, because neither is memoized. (3) The 200ms minDelayDone ActivityIndicator gate (lines 42/47-51/106) is a separate, unrelated fixed-cost UX affordance the code's own comment says masks a real one-frame remount glitch; it does not scale with data size and carries an unverified visual-regression risk if removed, so it is out of scope for this fix. Separately, downsampleData/downsampleDataPreserveEndpoints (lib/utils/downsample.ts) — written to shrink long series — is dead code (only its own test imports it), so nothing ever reduces the 365-point payload actually rendered by CartesianChart at 1Y.

Fix: Part A (launch-safe core, required): memoize yScaleInfo in useMemo keyed on [data, goal] (Graph1.tsx:74-103). Memoize chartKey in useMemo keyed on [data, chartColor, colors.textSecondary, colors.ringTrack] — KEEP the full per-point `day:value` join content unchanged (do not truncate to color-only): the per-point join is the only mechanism that repaints CartesianChart on interior-only data edits (parent's topSig key at progress.tsx:99 only encodes length + last day + last value and misses interior changes, e.g. correcting a past body-weight day or an old workout log's 1RM while a later real entry keeps length/last-point constant). Memoizing eliminates the actual 'every render' cost (recompute on unrelated re-renders) while preserving 100% of existing repaint/correctness behavior. Do NOT remove the 200ms minDelayDone gate as part of this fix; it is orthogonal to the O(n)-scaling root cause and its removal carries a documented, unverified visual-glitch risk — track as a separate follow-up gated on manual device QA. Part B (recommended, not required, ships only with these corrections): wire downsampleDataPreserveEndpoints into topRawData for the nutrition body-weight series at 6M/1Y only (progress.tsx topRawData, ~52 buckets, precision 1, aggregation 'avg'), with (i) pre-first-real-entry zero-fill placeholder points trimmed/excluded before downsampling so no bucket averages a 0 placeholder with a real weight, (ii) bucketFromPoints (downsample.ts:25-28) changed to a single representative 'M/D' label (bucket's last raw day) instead of 'first-last' concatenation, (iii) ALL label assertions across lib/utils/__tests__/downsample.test.ts updated (not just lines 33-98 — the shared helper also affects downsampleData's own concatenated-label assertions throughout the file, including the 'Date Label Formatting' block), and (iv) an explicit documented caveat that month-boundary ticks (Graph1.tsx monthTickValues, lines 59-63) will be approximately rather than exactly aligned to month starts post-downsampling (off by up to ~6 days), since bucket labels no longer sit on dense-daily 'M/1' points.

      Line 74-103: wrap yScaleInfo in useMemo keyed on [data, goal]. Line 39: wrap chartKey in useMemo keyed on [data, chartColor, colors.textSecondary, colors.ringTrack] — KEEP the full `data.map((d) => `${d.day}:${d.value}`).join('|')` content; do not reduce it to color-only.   - app/(tabs)/progress.tsx

Blast radius & safety:
  Callers:    Graph1 is rendered from app/(tabs)/progress.tsx (top line card, line 206, both lift 1RM and nutrition body-weight modes) and also from two __DEV__-gated dev-hub harnesses omitted from the original brief: components/devTest/LineChartTest.tsx:1 and components/devTest/mockups/scenes/ProgressScene.tsx:3/84 (keyed as `top-${mode}-${isDark}` on static canned data — never affected by the interior-edit correctness concern since its data never mutates).   Edge cases: Empty data (yScaleInfo already returns a default domain at line 75; downsample returns input unchanged when length <= targetCount, so short new-account series pass through untouched). Adjudication decisions:
  - Reducing chartKey to color-only (dropping the per-point day:value join) relies on the parent's topSig (progress.tsx:99 = `${length}:${lastDay}:${lastValue}`) to catch data changes, but topSig is lossy — it misses interior-point edits (e.g. correcting a past body-weight day, or an old workout log's 1RM) that leave length and the last point unchanged. This still eliminates the O(n) join recompute on every incidental re-render (press-drag state updates, containerWidth onLayout, font-readiness churn, the minDelay effect) — which is the actual 'every render' cost named in the root cause — without dropping the only mechanism that detects interior-only data changes.       -> MATERIAL — accepted. Dropping this timer does not address the stated root cause (the O(n) cost scales with account age; the 200ms delay is a fixed cost unrelated to data size), and the code's own comment is direct evidence of an unverified visual-regression risk. Removed from Part A's required scope. [Consolidated multi-agent brief (not yet adjudicated)]

Root cause: app/(tabs)/settings.tsx:42-60 polls `powerSync.getUploadQueueStats()` on a bare `useEffect(..., [])` + `setInterval(..., 1000)`. Because Expo Router's tab layout keeps every `Tabs.Screen` mounted for the whole session, this effect's cleanup only fires on unmount — never on tab blur — so once the user visits Settings, a 1-second SQLite query loop runs for the rest of the session even while Log/Progress are foregrounded. This is a fixed, day-one (not just degrading) CPU/battery drain and a recurring source of SQLite read contention. Unlike `SyncWatchdog` (components/GuardComponents/SyncWatchdog.tsx:13,23-30), which deliberately runs at a 30s cadence and skips work when the session isn't active, this poll has no focus- or activity-gating at all, and 1s is faster than the passive "Syncing N changes…" status line needs. A structurally identical (but drifted) copy of the same poll-then-map-then-setState pattern exists in the dev-only components/devTest/DevStatsModal.tsx:131-152 (which additionally tracks raw JSON/error/lastPolledAt and intentionally stays at 1s/always-on for debugging) — confirmed by direct read; that duplication is a maintenance/drift risk but is dev-only and zero-cost in production, so it does not itself justify inflating the scope of this fix.

Fix: Verified against the live file (app/(tabs)/settings.tsx lines 1-66): line 7 imports only `useRouter` from `expo-router`; line 9 imports `useEffect, useMemo, useState` from `react`, and `useEffect` is used nowhere else in the file (single match at line 42) so it can be dropped once replaced. `expo-router` does export `useFocusEffect` (re-exported from `./useFocusEffect` per node_modules/expo-router/build/exports.d.ts:21), so no new dependency is needed. `getPendingUploadEstimate` (lib/powersync/uploadQueueStats.ts:5-11) and `formatUploadQueueStatsRaw` are also called from lib/powersync/FlushUploads.ts:60-61 for the sign-out flush gate — untouched by this fix either way.

Agreed fix (smallest correct change): replace the mount-scoped `useEffect(() => {...}, [])` at settings.tsx:42-60 with `useFocusEffect(useCallback(() => {...}, []))`, keeping the existing `cancelled` flag, the immediate `void poll()`, the same `setInterval`, and the same cleanup (`cancelled = true; clearInterval(id)`) verbatim — `useFocusEffect`'s cleanup fires on blur (not just unmount), so the interval now starts when Settings gains focus and stops the instant it loses focus, eliminating the whole-session drain while the tab is not visible. Bump the interval from 1000ms to 3000ms in the same edit: the status line is a passive, non-interactive indicator, so sub-second freshness was never needed, and this is a zero-risk, zero-extra-code addition to the same block. Do NOT touch `getPendingUploadEstimate`, `getUploadQueueStats`, or the `syncStatusLine` memo — only the scheduling wrapper changes, so the rendered text/thresholds are byte-for-byte identical whenever the tab is focused.

An event-driven rewrite (subscribing to `powerSync.registerListener`/`currentStatus`) was considered and rejected: `currentStatus` exposes connected/uploading flags, not the queue count, and the count's field mapping is SDK-version-sensitive — more invasive for no correctness gain over focus-gating.

Structure's duplication concern (settings.tsx vs. DevStatsModal.tsx) is real but out of scope for the minimal M11 fix: DevStatsModal is __DEV__-gated, dev-only, has zero production cost, and deliberately keeps a different cadence/always-on intent for debugging — extracting a shared `lib/hooks/useUploadQueueEstimate.ts` hook (as one draft proposed) would touch 3 files instead of 1 and add new hook + test surface for a maintainability benefit only, not a correctness or performance fix to the production drain M11 is about. Per the rubric (correctness > safety > convention-fit > least-code > testability), the inline one-file fix is preferred as the required change. Recommend the hook extraction as an optional, separately-tracked follow-up (see "Conflicts resolved" below) so the two copies don't keep drifting, but do not block or expand this fix on it.

Files to change:
  - app/(tabs)/settings.tsx:42-60
      Replace the bare `useEffect(() => {...}, [])` polling block with `useFocusEffect(useCallback(() => {...}, []))`, keeping the identical body (cancelled flag, immediate void poll(), setInterval, cleanup). Change the interval delay from 1000 to 3000ms in the same edit.
  - app/(tabs)/settings.tsx:7

  - app/(tabs)/settings.tsx:9

Blast radius & safety:
  Callers:    SettingsScreen (the `(tabs)/settings` route rendered by app/(tabs)/_layout.tsx) is the only owner of this effect; nothing imports it. Offline/error: the existing catch still sets the estimate to null -> 'Checking sync…', unchanged. `getUploadQueueStats` returning an unrecognized shape still yields null via the untouched `getPendingUploadEstimate`. The only observable differences are (1) polling stops when the tab is not visible (where the value was never rendered anyway) and resumes immediately on refocus, and (2) refresh cadence goes from 1s to 3s while focused, which only affects the latency of a non-interactive, non-critical count. M12  (Medium)  [DONE]
===============================================================================
[Adjudicated final brief]

Root cause: Connector.uploadData (lib/powersync/Connector.ts:88-118) drains a single CRUD transaction's ops with one serial `await` per op, so every write costs a full Supabase HTTP round-trip even when many ops in the same local writeTransaction hit the same table. A single drag-reorder writes N `UPDATE "order"` rows in one writeTransaction (updateWorkoutOrders/updateExerciseOrders, powersyncStore.ts:159-169,262-272), which PowerSync surfaces as N independent PATCH ops (distinct id, distinct order value — there is no bulk-PATCH-with-distinct-values primitive, so these can never be collapsed into one HTTP call, only run concurrently). Bulk single-transaction writes (insertExercisesWithOrderBump, insertDuplicateWorkout) hit the same serial-RTT cost for their PUT/DELETE runs. Scope correction from the original analysis: uploadData drains exactly ONE local CRUD transaction per call (getNextCrudTransaction, line 81) and returns after transaction.complete(); autocommit writes like insertLog are each their own transaction, so this fix does NOT speed up cross-transaction multi-day offline backlogs (that would require getCrudBatch, left unchanged) — it only speeds up single large writeTransactions with many same-table ops. The sign-out flush gate (FlushUploads.ts) benefits only for that narrower case.

Fix: Do NOT batch ops into multi-row Supabase calls (no upsert(array), no .delete().in(ids)) — verification against Connector.ts and PostgREST semantics showed this introduces real, reachable regressions: duplicate-PK in a PUT run raises Postgres 21000, which isNonRetryableUploadError (line 15-24, regex /^2[23]/) does not classify as non-retryable, so it rethrows and the transaction retries forever, stalling the queue and the sign-out flush gate; heterogeneous per-row column sets in a PUT run break PostgREST's bulk upsert (PGRST102); .in(ids) DELETE batching can exceed URL/query-length limits on exactly the large-backlog case being optimized. Instead, wrap the EXISTING single-row createRecord/updateRecord/deleteRecord calls (unchanged signatures, unchanged prepareRecordForSupabase per-op transform at line 91, unchanged per-op try/catch + isNonRetryableUploadError dead-letter/Sentry logic at lines 93-117) in a bounded-concurrency pool: group transaction.crud into contiguous runs by (op, table) as before, but instead of buffering into an array payload, dispatch each op's existing single-row call concurrently up to a small named cap (e.g. a MAX_CONCURRENT_UPLOAD_OPS constant, not a magic number). Two safeguards: (1) settings and weight_progress ops (both PUT and PATCH — not just PUT) always execute strictly serially, never entering the concurrency pool, consistent with the existing SELF_HEALING_CONFLICT_TABLES carve-out (lines 6-10, this closes a gap in the original PATCH design); (2) if a run contains more than one op for the same row id (not guaranteed impossible by PowerSync's CRUD queue, and not something the connector should silently assume), those specific ops execute serially relative to each other in original order, while other distinct-id ops in the same run may still run concurrently. Each op's error handling is byte-identical to today — no new fallback path, no change to isNonRetryableUploadError.

Files to change:
  - lib/powersync/Connector.ts:80-122
      Replace the flat serial for-loop with: group transaction.crud into contiguous (op, table) runs; for settings/weight_progress runs (both PUT and PATCH), keep the existing strictly-serial per-op loop; for all other runs, dispatch the existing createRecord/updateRecord/deleteRecord calls through a bounded-concurrency pool (named cap constant), serializing any same-id repeats within a run; preserve the existing per-op try/catch + isNonRetryableUploadError dead-letter/rethrow logic unchanged for every op regardless of scheduling.
  - lib/powersync/Connector.ts
      No changes to createRecord/updateRecord/deleteRecord/prepareRecordForSupabase/isNonRetryableUploadError bodies — each op still performs the same single-row Supabase call it does today; only the scheduling around the loop changes.
  - lib/powersync/__tests__/connector.test.ts
      Add cases: N same-table PUT/PATCH/DELETE ops run concurrently up to the cap but still issue N distinct Supabase calls (not collapsed); per-op dead-letter/rethrow behavior is unchanged under concurrency; settings/weight_progress ops (PUT and PATCH) remain strictly serial; a run containing two ops for the same id serializes those two relative to each other while other ops in the run run concurrently; concurrency is capped (assert no more than the cap in flight at once). Remove/replace any 'batched -> single call' or 'collapsed calls' style expectations — they are not achievable and were factually wrong for the PATCH case.

Blast radius & safety:
  Callers:    uploadData is invoked only by PowerSync internals; its external contract is unchanged — upload every op in the transaction, then call transaction.complete(). FlushUploads.ts (sign-out gate) and SyncWatchdog benefit passively from faster drain on large single-transaction writes only (see rescoped rootCause). No app code imports uploadData directly. updateWorkoutOrders/updateExerciseOrders callers (WorkoutContext/index.tsx:148,281) are untouched.
  Edge cases: Empty transaction -> early return unchanged. Single-op transaction -> pool of 1, identical to current path. Mixed op types / cross-table FK order (insert workout before its exercises in insertDuplicateWorkout) -> preserved, since runs are still flushed in original contiguous-run order and only intra-run ops of the same (op,table) are concurrent. settings/weight_progress -> unchanged per-row pre-SELECT and now explicitly serial for PATCH too, so the 23505 self-healing retry path is intact for both PUT and PATCH. Poison op in a concurrent run -> isolated exactly as today (each op keeps its own try/catch/Sentry tag); no batch-level error class is ever introduced, so isNonRetryableUploadError needs no changes. Same-id repeat within a run -> serialized relative to each other, so ordering is preserved even though PowerSync's CRUD queue does not document a 'no duplicate id per transaction' guarantee. Large reorder -> concurrency is capped, avoiding PostgREST connection/rate-limit fan-out.
  Preserved:  No-error path yields byte-identical server state: every op still executes the exact same single-row Supabase call (upsert(record), update(...).eq('id',id), delete().eq('id',id)) it does today — only the scheduling (serial -> bounded-concurrent) changes, so there is no duplicate-PK 21000 risk, no heterogeneous-key PGRST102 risk, and no DELETE URL-length risk (all of which the original array-batching design would have introduced). Error path (dead-letter vs rethrow, Sentry tagging) is per-op and unchanged. This is a genuinely perf-only fix: no rendered value, no stored value, and no op count changes — only how many round-trips are in flight at once. The reorder headline case improves via latency overlap only, not request-count reduction — the brief and its tests state this accurately rather than claiming 'collapsed calls.'
  Tests:      Extend lib/powersync/__tests__/connector.test.ts per the files[] entry above. Run: npx jest lib/powersync/__tests__/connector.test.ts and the sibling flushUploads.test.ts / persistErrors.test.ts to confirm the sign-out flush gate and error paths still pass unchanged.
  DevHub:     approval=na; plan={"applicable":false,"naReason":"Backend PowerSync-Supabase upload scheduling change with no UI surface; not a Dev Hub preview candidate.","components":"","scenarios":[],"expected":"","testPage":""}

Adjudication decisions:
  - Headline drag-reorder case (updateWorkoutOrders/updateExerciseOrders, powersyncStore.ts:159-169,262-272) emits N distinct PATCH rows each with a unique id and a unique 'order' value; PostgREST/Supabase-js has no bulk-PATCH-with-distinct-values primitive, so Promise.all only parallelizes N HTTP requests, it never collapses them. The brief's own test-spec bullet 'N same-table PATCH reorder -> collapsed calls' and the framing 'perf-only fix... only how many round-trips carry them' overstate the win for the motivating example. (Multiple duplicate submissions of this same claim.)
      -> MATERIAL. Verified against updateRecord (Connector.ts:243-249) and the reorder writers, which confirm each row is independent with distinct id+order. Amended: rootCause/fix/blastRadius language and the test spec are corrected to say PATCH ops are run concurrently (bounded pool), not collapsed — wall-clock improves via RTT overlap, HTTP call count is unchanged. The false 'collapsed calls' test expectation is removed.  (Correctness (rule 1) — the brief's own claims must match what is actually achievable; a fix whose description doesn't match its real effect fails the gate even before code is written.)
  - Batched .upsert(array) is not equivalent to N single upserts when a contiguous PUT run contains two rows with the same primary key: Postgres raises 21000 ('ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE cannot affect row a second time', class 21). isNonRetryableUploadError only matches /^2[23]/ (Connector.ts:15-24), so a 21000 error is treated as retryable, rethrown, transaction.complete() is never reached, and PowerSync retries the same doomed transaction forever — stalling the queue and the sign-out flush gate (FlushUploads.ts:44-74). (Multiple duplicate submissions.)
      -> MATERIAL, verified — the classifier regex and error semantics are exactly as described. This is a real regression the current per-row loop never hits. Rather than patch around it (dedup-by-id, widen the classifier), the array-upsert mechanism is dropped entirely in favor of the reviewer's bounded-concurrency alternative (see below), which never constructs a multi-row payload and so cannot trigger 21000.  (Correctness (rule 1, hard gate) + Safety (rule 2) — introduces a queue-stall/regression the current code doesn't have.)
  - Batched array upsert(array) must still route each record through prepareRecordForSupabase (Connector.ts:65-74), or user_exercises.accessory_muscles (a JSON-string-to-Postgres-array conversion) breaks/corrupts on the batched path.
      -> MATERIAL as a preservation requirement. Moot under the adopted bounded-concurrency design: each op still calls the existing createRecord/updateRecord/deleteRecord (and the per-op prepareRecordForSupabase call at line 91) exactly as today, just scheduled concurrently instead of serially — so the transform is naturally preserved with zero extra code. Called out explicitly in the amended brief so the implementer doesn't accidentally build a raw-opData batch path.  (Correctness (rule 1) — resolved by design choice rather than by adding a patch.)
  - The stated fallback ('per-op fallback on non-retryable batch error') never fires for batch-level failures that aren't classified 22/23 (e.g. PostgREST PGRST102 heterogeneous-keys error, connection/rate-limit errors), so a structurally-doomed batch rethrows forever instead of isolating the poison op. (Multiple duplicate submissions.)
      -> MATERIAL, verified against the classifier. Resolved by design: the adopted bounded-concurrency approach never issues a multi-row batch call, so there is no 'batch error' needing a fallback — every op keeps its own single-row call and its own try/catch exactly as today (Connector.ts:93-117 loop body unchanged, only the scheduling around it changes).  (Correctness (rule 1) — resolved by dropping the batching layer that created the ambiguous failure mode.)
  - upsert(array) assumes every PUT row in a run shares an identical column-key set; PowerSync opData for two rows of the same table can differ in which nullable columns are present (e.g. workouts with/without note), and PostgREST's bulk insert/upsert rejects (or NULLs-out) mismatched key sets — a failure/data-loss mode the current per-row upsert never hits. (Multiple duplicate submissions.)
      -> MATERIAL, verified — PostgREST's documented bulk-upsert behavior requires a uniform column set across array rows. Resolved by design: the adopted bounded-concurrency approach keeps each row as its own single-row upsert(record) call (Connector.ts:181-188 unchanged), so heterogeneous key sets across rows are a non-issue.  (Correctness (rule 1) — a fix that trades a real, reachable failure mode for a perf win fails the gate; resolved by design choice.)
  - Batching DELETEs into .delete().in('id', ids) can exceed URL/query-length limits on exactly the large multi-day-backlog scenario the fix targets, since PostgREST encodes .in() filters into the request URL. (Multiple duplicate submissions.)
      -> MATERIAL, verified — Supabase-js/PostgREST filters are always query-string based regardless of HTTP verb, so an unbounded id list is a real availability risk. Resolved by design: the adopted bounded-concurrency approach keeps each delete as its own .delete().eq('id', id) call (Connector.ts:252-259 unchanged), only running them concurrently, so there is no URL-length concern and no chunk-size tuning needed.  (Correctness/Safety (rules 1-2) — resolved by dropping the .in(ids) batching mechanism entirely rather than adding a chunk cap.)
  - Promise.all-based concurrent PATCH breaks per-op dead-letter isolation and attribution: today's loop keys Sentry tagging and continue/rethrow off a specific op (table/opId/opType, Connector.ts:108-117); a batch-level failure (array upsert / .in(ids) / naive Promise.all) can't identify which row failed, and Promise.all rejects on first rejection, losing sibling results. (Multiple duplicate submissions.)
      -> MATERIAL, verified against the current per-op catch block. Resolved by design: keep each op's own try/catch exactly as today (unchanged isNonRetryableUploadError call with op.table, unchanged Sentry tags with op.table/op.op/op.id), and only change how many of these op-promises are in flight at once (bounded concurrency pool). Per-op dead-letter attribution is therefore byte-identical to today.  (Correctness (rule 1) + Testability (rule 5) — per-op error semantics must remain unit-testable exactly as before.)
  - Concurrent same-table PATCH via Promise.all has no invariant preventing two ops in one run from targeting the same row id; if that happens, network-completion order (not queue order) wins, which is a changed stored value, not a perf-only change. Nothing in the connector enforces the 'distinct ids' assumption the brief relies on — it currently only holds because the specific reorder callers happen to assign distinct ids. (Multiple duplicate submissions.)
      -> MATERIAL. The brief cannot assume an unenforced caller-side invariant for a connector-level change. Amended: the grouping/scheduling logic must detect same-id repeats within a run and force those specific ops to execute serially, in original order, relative to each other — while ops with distinct ids in the same run may still run concurrently (bounded pool). This is cheap insurance that removes the dependency on an unstated PowerSync CRUD-queue guarantee.  (Safety (rule 2) — smallest blast radius means not silently depending on an unproven assumption when a small, local safeguard (serialize by id) removes the risk entirely.)
  - Unbounded concurrency (naive Promise.all with no cap) means a single large reorder fans out to N simultaneous HTTP requests, which can exceed PostgREST connection/rate limits and produce new retryable errors — stalling the drain/flush gate rather than speeding it up.
      -> MATERIAL. Amended: the concurrency pool must use an explicit, named cap (a small constant, not a magic number) rather than unbounded Promise.all, so wall-clock improves without risking rate-limit/connection-pool errors on large transactions.  (Safety (rule 2) — bound the blast radius of concurrent load on the backend.)
  - Settings PATCH (Connector.ts:196-241, a read-modify-write on the single per-user settings row via getSession + select user_id + update-by-user_id) is not carved out of the concurrent-PATCH change — the brief only excludes settings/weight_progress from the PUT batching branch, not from PATCH concurrency, so concurrent settings PATCH ops (if PowerSync ever emits more than one in a transaction) would race on the same row.
      -> MATERIAL. Amended: extend the existing settings/weight_progress carve-out to cover BOTH PUT and PATCH — ops on these two tables always run strictly serially, never inside the bounded-concurrency pool, consistent with the SELF_HEALING_CONFLICT_TABLES rationale already documented at Connector.ts:6-10.  (Safety (rule 2) — preserve existing single-row race protection uniformly across op types, not just the one the author happened to think of.)
  - Root cause overstates the fix's reach: uploadData drains exactly one local CRUD transaction per invocation (getNextCrudTransaction, Connector.ts:81) and returns after transaction.complete(); many small autocommit writes (e.g. insertLog) are each their own transaction, so intra-transaction op-grouping does nothing for a 'multi-day offline backlog' of many separate transactions — only single large writeTransactions with many same-table ops (reorder, insertExercisesWithOrderBump, insertDuplicateWorkout) actually benefit. The brief lists 'multi-day offline backlogs drain at RTT-per-op' as something this change fixes, which it does not (that would require getCrudBatch, explicitly left unchanged).
      -> MATERIAL. Verified against getNextCrudTransaction/transaction.complete() at Connector.ts:81-121. Amended: rootCause is rescoped to say the fix speeds up single large local writeTransactions with many ops on the same table (drag-reorder, bulk exercise insert, duplicate-workout), not cross-transaction multi-day backlogs; the sign-out flush gate benefits only for that narrower case.  (Correctness (rule 1) — the root-cause statement itself must be factually accurate about what the fix does and does not address.)
  - A materially simpler correct approach exists: wrap the existing single-row createRecord/updateRecord/deleteRecord calls in a bounded-concurrency pool (Promise.all with a cap) instead of switching to upsert(array)/.in(ids). This recovers most of the wall-clock win without introducing duplicate-PK 21000 errors, heterogeneous-key PGRST102 rejections, DELETE URL-length risk, or per-op dead-letter attribution loss — each op keeps its own single-row Supabase call and its own error classification.
      -> ADOPTED as the core mechanism of the final brief, replacing the array-upsert/.in(ids)/naive-Promise.all design. This alternative independently resolves nearly every other material finding above (duplicate-PK, heterogeneous-keys, URL-length, dead-letter-attribution, fallback-ambiguity) by construction, is a smaller blast radius, reuses existing per-op helpers unchanged (better convention fit), and is less code than building array-batching plus all the safeguards needed to make it safe.  (Safety (rule 2) + Convention fit (rule 3) + Least code (rule 4) — all three favor the simpler design once rule 1 correctness issues rule out the original mechanism as specified; this is the reviewer's alternative, not an adjudicator-invented fix.)

M13  (Medium)  [DONE]
===============================================================================
[Adjudicated final brief]

Root cause: In app/workoutScreens/addExerciseModal.tsx, the filteredExercises useMemo (lines 46-50) depends directly on searchQuery, which the TextInput updates on every keystroke via onChangeText={setSearchQuery} (line 154). So fuse.search(q) — a synchronous Fuse scan over all 1,318 items with threshold 0.4, ignoreLocation — runs on the JS thread once per character, plus a full FlatList data swap each time, producing typing jank. The Fuse instance (line 39) and fullExerciseLibAsList (WorkoutContext/index.tsx:414) are already correctly memoized; the missing piece is a query debounce. A naive effect-driven debounce (as in the original banked brief, mirroring foodDBModal.tsx:73-90) introduces two new correctness defects confirmed against the actual code: (1) a stale-frame glitch on Clear because the memo and renderListEmpty would key off different state during the effect's post-paint gap, and (2) a false 'No results / Create X' prompt for up to ~200ms whenever the user edits away from a previously zero-result query toward one that actually matches, because renderListEmpty checks live searchQuery while filteredExercises lags on debouncedQuery. Both are fixed by keeping the empty/full-list short-circuit on the LIVE searchQuery (synchronous, same as today) and only deferring the actual fuse.search computation, plus gating the empty-state view on debounce settlement. A separate, pre-existing instance of the same class of bug exists in components/NeutralComponents/ScrollableList.tsx (used by progress.tsx's exercise picker, line 45: an unmemoized substring filter+sort over the same fullExerciseLibAsList on every keystroke, with no debounce at all) — this is out of scope for M13 as authored (rootCause and file list are bounded to addExerciseModal.tsx only) and is logged as a follow-up.

Fix: Add a debounced query that drives only the Fuse search step, while the filteredExercises memo continues to short-circuit synchronously on the live searchQuery for the empty case, and gate the 'No results' empty-state view on debounce settlement so it never renders against a live query the debounce hasn't caught up to yet.

Files to change:
  - app/workoutScreens/addExerciseModal.tsx
      Update the React import (line 14) to `{ useEffect, useMemo, useState }`. Add `const [debouncedQuery, setDebouncedQuery] = useState('')` near line 29. Add a plain uniform-debounce useEffect (no empty special-case): `useEffect(() => { const t = setTimeout(() => setDebouncedQuery(searchQuery), 200); return () => clearTimeout(t) }, [searchQuery])`. Rewrite the filteredExercises memo (lines 46-50) to short-circuit on the LIVE searchQuery for empty, and only use debouncedQuery for the actual Fuse call: `const filteredExercises = useMemo(() => { const liveQ = searchQuery.trim(); if (!liveQ) return fullExerciseLibAsList; const dq = debouncedQuery.trim(); return dq ? fuse.search(dq).map(r => r.item) : fullExerciseLibAsList }, [fuse, searchQuery, debouncedQuery, fullExerciseLibAsList])`. Update renderListEmpty (lines 170-172) to gate on settlement, not just live-query non-emptiness: only render the empty/create-prompt view when `debouncedQuery.trim() === searchQuery.trim() && filteredExercises.length === 0`; otherwise return null (covers both the not-yet-settled window and the already-handled empty-query case). Leave TextInput value, openCreateSheet, and handleCreateAndStage's `setSearchQuery('')` untouched — the memo's live-query short-circuit means clearing is instant with no dependency on debouncedQuery.
  - app/workoutScreens/__tests__/addExerciseModal.test.tsx
      New test (or add to existing workout-modal suite) using jest fake timers + @testing-library/react-native, mocking useWorkout with a small fullExerciseLibAsList: (1) rapid onChangeText calls do not change rendered Fuse-filtered results until the 200ms timer fires; (2) clearing the field restores the full list in the same render/act() as the clear, with no dependency on advancing timers (pins the synchronous short-circuit); (3) regression test for the false-empty-state bug — settle a query with zero matches (e.g. 'zzz'), confirm the empty/create-prompt view renders, then type a query with real matches (e.g. 'bench') and assert the empty/create-prompt view does NOT render before the 200ms timer advances (pins the debounce-settlement gate on renderListEmpty).

Blast radius & safety:
  Callers:    addExerciseModal is the sole consumer of this fuse/filter; it is reached only as a workout-screen modal (navigated from a workout via workoutId param). fullExerciseLibAsList is also read by app/(tabs)/progress.tsx:74, but that screen runs its own filter inside components/NeutralComponents/ScrollableList.tsx (a separate, unmemoized substring filter, confirmed at line 45-51) — it does not touch this modal's memo or state, so this change is fully contained to addExerciseModal.tsx. ScrollableList.tsx's own version of the same class of bug is a known, logged follow-up, not part of this fix's blast radius.
  Edge cases: Empty query / Clear button: filteredExercises short-circuits on the live searchQuery (not debouncedQuery), so the full 1,318-item list returns in the same render as the clear tap or last-char delete — truly synchronous, no post-paint effect on the critical path. Zero-result-to-match transition (the defect reviewers surfaced): renderListEmpty now requires debouncedQuery to have caught up to the live searchQuery before showing 'No results' / 'Create X', so a user editing away from a no-match query toward a real match never sees a false empty state or false create-prompt mid-debounce. Create-flow: openCreateSheet seeds createName from the live searchQuery; handleCreateAndStage's setSearchQuery('') hits the same live-query short-circuit, resetting the list instantly. Rapid unmount (modal dismiss mid-type): the effect's clearTimeout cleanup cancels any pending timer — no setState-after-unmount.
  Preserved:  The fix changes only WHEN Fuse runs, never WHAT it returns for a settled query: Fuse instance, keys, threshold, ignoreLocation, and fullExerciseLibAsList input are untouched, so for any query the user stops typing on, the result set is identical to today's. Unlike the original banked design, the amended memo also preserves today's exact synchronous behavior for the empty-query case and eliminates two new visual defects (stale-list flash on Clear, false empty-state flash) that the naive effect-only design would have introduced.
  Tests:      New app/workoutScreens/__tests__/addExerciseModal.test.tsx as amended above (debounce-delay assertion, synchronous-clear assertion, and the zero-result-to-match false-empty-state regression test). Run the full suite via npm run test:ci to confirm no regression. Manual QA: type a multi-word exercise name quickly and confirm smooth typing with results settling after the pause; tap Clear and confirm instant snap to full list with no flash of stale results; type a no-match string then edit toward a real exercise name and confirm no false 'No results / Create' flash appears mid-edit — verify in both dark and light per CLAUDE.md.

Adjudication decisions:
  - Mirroring foodDBModal.tsx:73-90 literally is a trap because that effect's `if (!q) return` never resets result state itself — the empty-reset lives in a separate onChangeText handler (handleQueryChange), which addExerciseModal doesn't have.
      -> Material ambiguity in the instruction. Resolved by amending the fix design so the debounce effect no longer needs any empty-special-case at all: the `filteredExercises` memo short-circuits on the LIVE `searchQuery` (not `debouncedQuery`) for the empty branch, so clearing is handled by the memo itself, synchronously, regardless of what the debounce effect is doing. The effect becomes a plain, uniform debounce with no branching — removing the risk that an implementer copies the literal foodDBModal effect and leaves debouncedQuery stuck after Clear.  (Correctness (rule 1) / Safety (rule 2) — an ambiguous 'mirror X' instruction that omits X's actual reset mechanism is a real defect risk, not a nitpick.)
  - Clearing the field cannot be 'synchronous' with an effect-driven debouncedQuery: for one painted frame, filteredExercises still holds the previous non-empty search's results while the input shows empty, before the effect resolves debouncedQuery to ''.
      -> Material — confirmed by tracing the render sequence (setSearchQuery('') updates render immediately; debouncedQuery only updates in a post-paint effect). Resolved by the same memo redesign: short-circuiting on live `searchQuery` for the empty case makes the full-list restore happen in the exact same render as the keystroke/clear-tap, eliminating the stale frame entirely — no effect race is on the critical path for the empty case.  (Correctness (rule 1) — the brief's stated guarantee ('restores synchronously') must actually hold; Safety (rule 2) — no new visible glitch vs. today's synchronous memo.)
  - Repointing the entire memo to debouncedQuery produces the same one-frame stale-list bug on Clear; reviewer proposes short-circuiting empty on the live searchQuery and deferring only the non-empty fuse.search via debouncedQuery.
      -> Adopted verbatim as the design for the amended fix — this is strictly simpler than an effect that must special-case empty, and it is what actually satisfies the brief's own edge-case promises.  (Correctness (rule 1), then Least code (rule 4) — the reviewer's alternative is both more correct and smaller than the original effect-reset approach.)
  - The proposed 'clearing restores synchronously' test can't catch the one-frame stale bug because @testing-library/react-native flushes passive effects inside act(), so it would pass regardless of the on-device glitch.
      -> Immaterial once the memo redesign is adopted: since Clear no longer depends on the effect at all (memo short-circuits on live searchQuery in the same render), the underlying bug the reviewer worried the test couldn't catch no longer exists, so the test assertion becomes a true, meaningful guarantee rather than a false-confidence one. No change needed to the test's intent beyond keeping it.  (Testability (rule 5) — moot because rule 1 was already satisfied by the design fix; the test now validates a real invariant.)
  - The identical per-keystroke sync-scan-over-1,318-items symptom exists in progress.tsx's exercise picker via ScrollableList.tsx (line 45, unmemoized substring filter+sort on every keystroke, no debounce at all), so a modal-only fix leaves half the hot path unaddressed.
      -> Material as a genuine parallel defect (verified: ScrollableList.tsx runs `data.filter().sort()` inline on every render with no useMemo or debounce, and progress.tsx feeds it the same fullExerciseLibAsList at line 74) — but it is a distinct component, distinct screen, and outside the rootCause this brief was scoped to (rootCause text names only addExerciseModal.tsx). Folding it in would double the blast radius into a shared, multi-consumer component (ScrollableList is used across macro pickers etc.) for a fix explicitly scoped elsewhere. Kept out of M13; logged as a recommended follow-up ticket rather than invented scope creep.  (Safety / blast radius (rule 2) — smallest blast radius wins; correctness of M13 as authored is unaffected since its rootCause never claimed to cover ScrollableList.tsx.)
  - Because renderListEmpty keys off live searchQuery while filteredExercises lags on debouncedQuery, a query that previously settled to zero results (e.g. 'zzz') followed by editing toward a query that DOES match (e.g. 'bench') will show a false 'No results for bench' + 'Create bench' prompt for the ~200ms the debounce is still catching up — contradicting the brief's own 'no premature No results flash' claim.
      -> Material and the most severe finding — this is a real regression versus today's synchronous behavior (today's memo never shows an empty state for a query it hasn't actually searched) and could prompt users to create duplicate custom exercises for items that exist. Fixed by gating renderListEmpty on settlement, not just non-emptiness: only render the empty/create-prompt view when `debouncedQuery.trim() === searchQuery.trim() && filteredExercises.length === 0` (i.e., the debounce has caught up to what's on screen). Add a fake-timer regression test for this exact zero-result-then-matching-query transition.  (Correctness (rule 1, HARD GATE) — a fix that introduces a new false-empty-state bug does not fully resolve the issue cleanly and must be amended before it can be accepted.)
  - The proposed useEffect for the debounce requires `useEffect`, but addExerciseModal.tsx currently imports only `{ useMemo, useState }` from 'react' (line 14) — the brief's file-change list omits updating this import.
      -> Material, trivial to fix. Amended file-change instructions now explicitly include updating the React import to `{ useEffect, useMemo, useState }`.  (Correctness (rule 1) — code as literally described would fail to compile without this.)
